Green’s function estimates for quasi-periodic operators on ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d} with power-law long-range hopping
Yunfeng Shi, Li Wen

Abstract
. We establish quantitative Green’s function estimates for a class of quasi-periodic (QP) operators on ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d} with power-law long-range hopping and analytic cosine type potentials. As applications, we prove the arithmetic version of localization, the finite volume version of ( 1 2 − ) (\frac{1}{2}-) -Hölder continuity of the IDS, and the absence of eigenvalues (for Aubry dual operators).



1.
Introduction

In this paper, we are concerned with QP operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\theta)=\varepsilon\mathcal{W}_{\phi}+v(\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})\delta_{\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime}},\ \bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},

(1.1)

where the off-diagonal part (i.e., the hopping term)
\mathcal{W}_{\phi}
is a Toeplitz operator satisfying

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{\phi}\psi)(\bm{n})=\sum_{\bm{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\phi(\bm{n}-\bm{l})\psi(\bm{l}),\ \phi(\bm{0})=0,\ |\phi(\bm{n})|\leq(1+\|\bm{n}\|)^{-\alpha}

(1.2)

with some
\alpha>0
and
\|\bm{n}\|=\sup\limits_{1\leq i\leq d}|n_{i}|
. The potential
v
is an analytic function defined on

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{R}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}:\ |\Im z|\leq R\},\ R>0,

satisfying for some positive constants
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\|z_{1}+z_{2}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq|v(z_{1})-v(z_{2})|\leq\kappa_{2}\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\|z_{1}+z_{2}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\ {\rm for}\ \forall z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{D}_{R},

(1.3)

where

\displaystyle\|z\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\sqrt{\|\Re z\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{2}+|\Im z|^{2}},\ \|x\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\inf_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}|l-x|\ {\rm for}\ x\in\mathbb{R}.

We let
\theta\in\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
,
\bm{\omega}\in[0,1]^{d}
, and
\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}n_{i}\omega_{i}.

In the following, we assume that
\bm{\omega}\in{DC}_{\tau,\gamma}
for some
\tau>d
,
\gamma>0
with

\displaystyle{DC}_{\tau,\gamma}=\left\{\bm{\omega}\in[0,1]^{d}:\ \|\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{\gamma}{\|\bm{n}\|^{\tau}}\ {\rm for}\ \forall\bm{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\right\}.

(1.4)

The special case of operators with
\mathcal{W}_{\phi}(m,n)=\delta_{|m-n|,1}
and
v=\cos 2\pi\theta
corresponds to the famous almost Mathieu operator (AMO).

The present work aims to establish quantitative Green’s function estimates for (
1.1
) via the multi-scale analysis (MSA) induction. As applications, the arithmetic localization, Hölder continuity of the IDS and absence of eigenvalues (for Aubry dual operators of (
1.1
)) are proved.

Over the past decades, the study of spectral and dynamical properties of discrete QP Schrödinger operators has been one of the central themes in mathematical physics. Of particular importance is the phenomenon of Anderson localization (i.e., the pure point spectrum with exponentially decaying eigenfunctions). Determining the nature of the spectrum and the eigenfunctions information of QP Schrödinger operators can be viewed as a small divisor problem, and substantial progress has been made following Green’s function estimates method based on MSA of Fröhlich-Spencer
[
FS83
]
.

In 2000, Bourgain
[
Bou00
]
first proved the
(\frac{1}{2}-)
-Hölder continuity of the IDS for AMO in the perturbative regime via MSA type Green’s function estimates. This result is remarkable since the important work of Goldstein-Schlag
[
GS01
]
which is non-perturbative and applies to more general QP potentials does not seem to provide explicit information on the Hölder exponent on the regularity of the IDS at that time.
1
1

1

Until 2009, Amor
[
Amo09
]
obtained the sharp
\frac{1}{2}
-Hölder continuity of the IDS for one-dimensional QP Schrödinger
operators with small analytic potentials by using the KAM reducibility method of Eliasson
[
Eli92
]
(cf.
[
AJ10
]
by Avila-Jitomirskaya for a nonperturbative extension).
However, the main contribution of
[
Bou00
]
may be its method: Bourgain established quantitative Green’s function estimates via MSA scheme, and the resonances there can be completely described by a pair of zeros of the Dirichlet determinant via the Weierstrass preparation theorem. The main idea originates from
[
Bou97
]
in the area of KAM theory for Hamiltonian systems, in which Bourgain revisited the Melnikov’s persistency problem and removed the second Melnikov’s conditions by using Green’s function estimates method based on the preparation type theorem. This method (combined with ideas of Craig-Wayne
[
CW93
]
) was later significantly extended in the breakthrough work
[
Bou98
]
to prove the existence of QP solutions for a class of 2-dimensional nonlinear Schrödinger equations (NLS). Definitely, the adaptation of this method to the field of spectral theory of QP Schrödinger operators requires to deal with
energy parameters
that do not appear in the nonlinear KAM setting. It is significantly difficult to eliminate resonances between frequencies and energies. This task becomes more challenging when dealing with general QP potentials even in the one-dimensional case (cf. Goldstein-Schlag
[
GS08
]
).

Bourgain’s approach in
[
Bou00
]
does not restrict only to the proof of Hölder continuity of the IDS for AMO. Indeed, in
[
Bou00
]
, Bourgain remarked that “
In fact, from this Green’s function result, one may also recover the Anderson localization results from
[
Sin87
,
FSW90
]
in the perturbative case. Our method is significantly different in the sense that it relies little on eigenvalues and eigenvalue perturbation theory
.” However, it is nontrivial to prove Anderson localization via directly using estimates of
[
Bou00
]
: (1). The oﬀ-diagonal elements of Green’s function in
[
Bou00
]
are only sub-exponentially decaying, which is not sufficient for the proof of Anderson localization; (2). To prove Anderson localization, one has to eliminate the energy
E\in\mathbb{R}
in Green’s function estimates by removing additional
\theta
. In this step, the
symmetry
property (which was unknown in
[
Bou00
]
) of the two zeros (depending on
E
) of the Dirichlet determinant becomes essential. Very recently, in
[
CSZ24b
]
, Cao-Shi-Zhang extended fully the method of Bourgain
[
Bou00
]
to prove the arithmetic Anderson localization (i.e., there is certain arithmetic description on the set of
\theta,\bm{\omega}
on which the Anderson localization holds true; cf.
[
CSZ24b
]
for details) for QP Schrödinger operators on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with the cosine potential. Related results were previously obtained by Jitomirskaya-Kachkovskiy
[
JK16
]
and Ge-You
[
GY20
]
via the reducibility-localization method based on Aubry duality. In
[
CSZ24b
]
, the authors reconstructed in each MSA induction step enlarged resonant blocks satisfying both the symmetry and translation invariance properties. This suffices for eliminating energies and double resonances, then the proof of Anderson localization. However, the arguments of
[
CSZ24b
]
rely crucially on the fast
exponential
decay of the long-range hopping and cannot apply to the power-law one (cf.
[
Shi22
,
Liu22
]
for results on more general sub-exponential long-range hopping QP operators). In addition, they only considered the cosine potential in
[
CSZ24b
]
and it is desirable to remove this restriction. Subsequently, Cao-Shi-Zhang
[
CSZ23
,
CSZ24a
]
have successfully extended the results of
[
CSZ24b
]
to the more general
C^{2}
-cosine type potentials via MSA type Green’s function estimates based on eigenvalues and eigenvalue perturbation theory, but required the QP operators to be self-adjoint (cf. Liu
[
Liu22
]
for recent results on the study of non-self-adjoint QP operators). The present work extends the method
[
CSZ24b
]
to handle non-self-adjoint QP operators with both power-law long-range hopping and more general analytic cosine type potentials.

Back to the KAM setting, the result of Bourgain
[
Bou98
]
was later essentially extended (cf. chapters 19 and 20 in
[
Bou05
]
) to both NLS and nonlinear wave equations of
arbitrary
space dimensions by establishing large deviation type Green’s function estimates, which build on semi-algebraic geometry arguments and matrix-valued Cartan’s estimates originated from the Anderson localization theory of QP Schrödinger operators on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by Bourgain-Goldstein-Schlag
[
BGS02
]
(cf. Bourgain
[
Bou07
]
and Jitomirskaya-Liu-Shi
[
JLS20
]
for the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
case). In order to apply semi-algebraic geometry method, the nonlinearity of PDEs in
[
Bou05
]
admits some special form of the polynomial type. Moreover, due to the weak separation property of resonant clusters induced by the normal frequencies, the QP solutions obtained in
[
Bou98
,
Bou05
]
are at most Gevrey regular in both time and space variables. Later in
[
BB13
]
, Berti-Bolle developed a novel approach again based on the Nash-Moser iteration and MSA type Green’s function estimates to prove the existence of Sobolev type QP solutions for a class of higher dimensional NLS with the finitely differentiable nonlinearity. Among others, the work
[
BB13
]
has to establish off-diagonal decay estimates on the Green’s function of linearized operators with power-law long-range hopping rather than the (sub)exponential or finite-range ones. For this purpose, Berti-Bolle proved a key coupling lemma to achieve power-law off-diagonal decay estimates of Green’s functions. The essential point of Berti-Bolle’s proof lies in the usage of
tame
property induced by the power-law weight, and this property is definitely invalid for the (sub)exponential weight. Based on ideas of
[
BB13
]
, a series of results
[
BB12
,
BB13
,
BCP15
,
BB20
]
toward the construction of Sobolev QP solutions for higher dimensional Hamiltonian PDEs have been obtained. As mentioned above, there are important connections between the two areas of Anderson localization and nonlinear KAM theory in the exponential (or analytic) perturbations case. In
[
BB20
]
, Berti-Bolle emphasized that “
The techniques developed in this monograph have deep connections with those used in Anderson-localization theory and we hope that the detailed presentation in this manuscript of all technical aspects of the proofs will allow a deeper interchange between the Anderson-localization and KAM for PDEs scientific communities
.” It is the other motivation of the present work that studies spectral problems via the adaptation of techniques initially developed in the community of KAM for PDEs and tries to address the above Berti-Bolle’s problem.

In
[
Shi21
]
, Shi first introduced the estimates of
[
BB13
]
in the area of random Schrödinger operators and proved localization for random operators with power-law long-range hopping and the Hölder continuity distribution (maybe singularly continuous). Previously, in the influential work
[
AM93
]
, Aizenman-Molchanov first proved the power-law localization via the the famous fractional moment method, which requires the absolute continuity of the random distribution when applying Simon-Wolff criterion
[
SW86
]
. Recently, Shi
[
Shi23
]
developed a Nash-Moser iteration type diagonalization method to prove localization for power-law long-range QP operators with monotone potentials in the absence of resonances, which plays an important role in the study of quantum suppression of chaos concerning non-analytic quantum kicked rotor (cf.
[
SW23
]
). The present paper extends the method of
[
Shi21
,
CSZ24b
]
further to investigate the power-law long-range QP operators in the presence of resonances.

1.1.
Main results

The main result of this paper is a quantitative version of Green’s function estimates.

We first introduce the function class of potentials. For
R>0
, let
\mathscr{V}_{R}
denote the set of analytic functions
v
on
\mathbb{D}_{R}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}:\ |\Im z|\leq R\}
satisfying (
1.3
). Let
|v|_{R}=\sup\limits_{z\in\mathbb{D}_{R}}|v(z)|
for
v\in\mathscr{V}_{R}
and
\mathscr{V}=\bigcup_{R>0}\mathscr{V}_{R}
.

Remark 1.1
.

The following functions belong to
\mathscr{V}
(cf. the Appendix
A
for a detailed proof):

•

Example 1
:

\displaystyle v_{1}(z)=\cos 2\pi z+\lambda_{2}\cos^{2}2\pi z+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\cos^{n}2\pi z,

where
2|\lambda_{2}|+\cdots+n|\lambda_{n}|<1
.

•

Example 2
:

\displaystyle v_{2}(z)=\cos 2\pi z+\epsilon f(z),

where
f
is any even function defined on
\mathbb{D}_{R}
and
0<|\epsilon|\leq\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(f)\ll 1
.

Given
E\in\mathbb{C}
and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, the Green’s function (if exists) is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}(E;\theta)=(\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}(\theta)-E)^{-1},\ \mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}(\theta)=\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{H}(\theta)\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda},

where
\mathcal{H}(\theta)
is given by (
1.1
) and
\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda}
denotes the restriction operator.

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denote the standard inner on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
and let
\mathcal{M}
be a bounded linear operator on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
. Write
\mathcal{M}(\bm{m},\bm{n})=\langle\delta_{\bm{m}},\mathcal{M}\delta_{\bm{n}}\rangle.
Define for all
\alpha\geq 0
the Sobolev norm as

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha}=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left(\sup_{\bm{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\mathcal{M}(\bm{k}+\bm{l},\bm{l})|\right)(1+\|\bm{k}\|)^{\alpha}.

(1.5)

Typically, we denote by
{\rm dist}(\cdot,\cdot)
(resp.
{\rm diam}(\cdot)
) the distance (resp. the diameter) induced by the norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Recall that
\bm{\omega}\in DC_{\tau,\gamma}
. At the
s
-th iteration step, let
\delta_{s}^{-1}
(resp.
N_{s}
) describe the resonance strength (resp. the size of resonant blocks) defined by

\displaystyle N_{s+1}=\left[\left(\frac{\gamma}{\delta_{s}}\right)^{\frac{1}{30\tau}}\right],\ \frac{\gamma}{\delta_{s+1}}=\left(\frac{\gamma}{\delta_{s}}\right)^{30},\ \delta_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{30}},

where
[x]
denotes the integer part of
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Then we have

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\bm{\omega}\in DC_{\tau,\gamma}
. Fix
d<\alpha_{0}<\tau
and
\alpha_{1}>2200\tau
. Let

\displaystyle v\in\mathscr{V}_{R},\ \|\mathcal{W}_{\phi}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}<+\infty.

Then there is some
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0},d,\tau,\gamma,v,R,\phi)>0
so that for
0<|\varepsilon|\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and
E\in v(\mathbb{D}_{R/2})
, there exists a sequence

\displaystyle\{\theta_{s}=\theta_{s}(E)\}_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{C}\ (s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\})

with the following properties: Fix any
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
, if a subset
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is
s
-
{\rm good}
(cf.
(\bm{e})_{s}
of Statement
4.1
for the definition of
s
-
{\rm good}
set, and Section
4
for the definitions of
\{\theta_{s}\}_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}
, sets
P_{s}
,
Q_{s}
,
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}
and
\zeta_{s}>0
), then

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}(E;\theta)\|_{0}

\displaystyle<\delta_{s}^{-\frac{2}{15}}\times\sup\limits_{\{\bm{k}\in P_{s}:\ \tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\Lambda\}}(\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}),

and for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}],

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}(E;\theta)\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle<\zeta_{s}^{\alpha}\delta_{s}^{-\frac{14}{3}}.

(1.6)

In particular, for any finite set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, there exists some
\tilde{\Lambda}
satisfying

\displaystyle\Lambda\subset\tilde{\Lambda}\subset\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ {\rm dist}(\bm{k},\Lambda)\leq 50N_{s}^{5}\}

(1.7)

so that if

\displaystyle\min_{\bm{k}\in\tilde{\Lambda}^{*}}\min_{\sigma=\pm 1}(\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\sigma\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}})>\delta_{s},

then

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Lambda}}^{-1}(E;\theta)\|_{0}

\displaystyle<\delta_{s}^{-\frac{32}{15}},

and for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}],

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Lambda}}^{-1}(E;\theta)\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle<\zeta_{s}^{\alpha}\delta_{s}^{-\frac{14}{3}},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\Lambda}^{*}=\left\{\bm{k}\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ {\rm dist}(\bm{k},\tilde{\Lambda})\leq\frac{1}{2}\right\}.

Remark 1.2
.

•

In (
1.6
), we have

{\rm diam}(\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s})=\zeta_{s}\ll({\rm diam}(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}))^{2/3}\leq({\rm diam}(\Lambda))^{2/3},

which leads to power-law off-diagonal decay of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}(E;\theta).

•

The lower bound
\alpha_{1}>2200\tau
may be improved.

•

Let us refer to Section
4
for a complete description of Green’s function estimates.

1.1.1.
Arithmetic localization

In this part, we state our arithmetic type localization results. We assume
\mathcal{H}(\theta)
is self-adjoint for
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
.

We first introduce the power-law (spectral) localization result.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
d<\tau_{1}<\tau
and define

\displaystyle\Theta_{\tau_{1}}=\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\ \|2\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq\frac{1}{\|\bm{n}\|^{\tau_{1}}}\ {\rm holds\ for\ finitely\ many\ }\bm{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
,
\mathcal{H}(\theta)
has pure point spectrum with power-law decay eigenfunctions (i.e., power-law localization) for
\theta\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\Theta_{\tau_{1}}
.

Remark 1.3
.

The investigations of the localization for QP Schrödinger operators have attracted great attention in both one dimension (cf. e.g.,
[
FSW90
,
Jit94
,
Eli97
,
Jit99
,
BG00
,
BJ02
,
Kle05
,
AYZ17
,
JL18
,
JSY19
,
HS22
,
Liu23
,
JL24
,
Han24
,
GJ24
,
FV25
]
) and higher dimensions (cf. e.g.,
[
CD93
,
Din97
,
BGS02
,
Bou07
,
JLS20
,
GY20
,
Shi22
,
Liu22
,
GYZ23
,
CSZ23
,
CSZ24b
,
CSZ24a
]
).

Our result on dynamical localization is

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
d<\tau_{1}<\tau
and define for
A\in(0,1),

\displaystyle\Theta_{\tau_{1},A}^{*}=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\ \|2\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}\|_{\mathbb{T}}>\frac{A}{\|\bm{n}\|^{\tau_{1}}}\ {\rm for}\ \forall\bm{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\right\}.

(1.8)

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, we have for

p\in(0,\frac{\alpha_{1}}{60}-2d)
and
\theta\in\Theta_{\tau_{1},A}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{\bm{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(1+\|\bm{n}\|)^{p}|\langle e^{\sqrt{-1}t\mathcal{H}(\theta)}\delta_{\bm{0}},\delta_{\bm{n}}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\max(A^{-\frac{29(p+2d)}{\tau}},\varepsilon_{0}^{-\frac{29(p+2d)}{\tau}}),

where
C=C(\alpha_{1},p,d)>0.

Remark 1.4
.

For more results on the study of dynamical localization for QP operators, we refer to
[
CSZ23
,
CSZ24a
]
and references therein.

1.1.2.
\left(\frac{1}{2}-\right)
-Hölder continuity of the IDS

We now consider the finite volume version of Hölder continuity of the IDS for
\mathcal{H}(\theta)
. We also assume
\mathcal{H}(\theta)
is self-adjoint for
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
.

For a finite set
\Lambda
, denote by
\#\Lambda
its cardinality. Let

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{\Lambda}(E;\theta)=\frac{1}{\#\Lambda}\#\{\lambda\in\sigma(\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}(\theta)):\ \lambda\leq E\}

and define the IDS as

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}(E)=\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathscr{N}_{\Lambda_{N}}(E;\theta),

(1.9)

where
\Lambda_{N}=\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \|\bm{k}\|\leq N\}
for
N>0
. It is well known that the limit in (
1.9
) exists and is independent of
\theta
for a.e.
\theta
.

We have

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\mu\in\left[\frac{3400\tau}{2\alpha_{1}+3097\tau},\frac{1}{2}\right)
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, we have for
0<\eta<\eta_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0},d,\tau,\gamma,v,R,\phi,\mu)
and sufficiently large
N
(depending on
\eta
),

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{T},\ E\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathscr{N}_{\Lambda_{N}}(E+\eta;\theta)-\mathscr{N}_{\Lambda_{N}}(E-\eta;\theta))\leq\eta^{\frac{1}{2}-\mu}.

In particular, the IDS is
(\frac{1}{2}-\mu)
-Hölder continuous.

Remark 1.5
.

We refer to
[
Liu22
,
CSZ23
,
CSZ24b
,
CSZ24a
]
and references therein for more results on the study of regularity of the IDS.

1.1.3.
Absence of eigenvalues

Let

\displaystyle h:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}=\mathbb{T}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

and let
\hat{h}=\{\hat{h}(\bm{n})\}_{\bm{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\hat{h}(\bm{n})=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}h(\bm{\theta})e^{-2\pi\sqrt{-1}\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\theta}}d\bm{\theta}.

We study the Aubry dual operators of (
1.1
), which read as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{H}}(\bm{x})=\mathcal{W}_{\hat{v}}+\varepsilon u(\bm{x}+l\bm{\omega})\delta_{l,l^{\prime}},\ \bm{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\ l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z},

(1.10)

where
u(\bm{x})=\sum\limits_{\bm{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\phi(\bm{n})e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}\bm{n}\cdot\bm{x}}.

We have

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}(\bm{x})
be defined by (
1.10
). Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, we have that
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}(\bm{x})
has no eigenvalues for all
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Remark 1.6
.

The operator
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}(\bm{x})
can be non-self-adjoint with the exponential long-range hopping. The potential
u
in (
1.10
) is finitely differentiable. This theorem extends the result of
[
Shi22
]
from Gevrey regular potentials to the finitely smooth ones permitting a fixed Diophantine frequency.

1.2.
Ideas of the proof and new ingredients

Our proof of Theorem
1.1
is based on a MSA type induction and combines ideas from
[
Bou00
,
CSZ24b
,
Shi21
,
BB13
]
. Once Theorem
1.1
was established, the proofs of arithmetic localization, Hölder continuity of the IDS and absence of eigenvalues just follow from standard arguments.

Fix
\theta\in\mathbb{D}_{R/2},\ E\in v(\mathbb{D}_{R/2}).

We use a MSA induction scheme to establish quantitative estimates on Green’s functions. Of particular importance is the analysis of resonances, which will be described by zeros of certain functions appearing as perturbations of some quadratic polynomials. Roughly speaking, at the
s
-th iteration step, the set
Q_{s}\subset\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
of singular sites will be described by a pair of symmetric zeros of certain functions, i.e.,

\displaystyle Q_{s}=\{\bm{k}\in P_{s}:\ \min_{\sigma=\pm 1}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\sigma\theta_{s}(E)\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{s}\}.

While Green’s functions restricted on
Q_{s}
can not be generally well controlled, the algebraic structure of
Q_{s}
combined with the Diophantine condition of
\bm{\omega}
can lead to fine separation property of singular sites. As a result, one can cover
Q_{s}
with a new generation of resonant blocks
{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s+1}\ (\bm{k}\in P_{s+1})
and enlarged resonant blocks
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s+1}\ (\bm{k}\in P_{s+1})
. It turns out that one can control
\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s+1}}^{-1}(E;\theta)\|_{0}
via zeros
\pm\theta_{s+1}(E)
of some new functions which are also perturbations of quadratic polynomials in the sense that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s+1}}^{-1}(E;\theta)\|_{0}<\delta_{s+1}^{-\frac{1}{15}}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{s+1}(E)\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{s+1}(E)\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}.

The key point is that some
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s+1}}^{-1}(E;\theta)
may become controllable at the
(s+1)
-th step while
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s+1}
intersects
Q_{s}
. Moreover, the completely uncontrollable singular sites form the
(s+1)
-th singular ones, i.e.,

\displaystyle Q_{s+1}=\{\bm{k}\in P_{s+1}:\ \min_{\sigma=\pm 1}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\sigma\theta_{s+1}(E)\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{s+1}\}.

The reduction of finding zeros (i.e.,
\pm\theta_{s}(E)
) of the Dirichlet determinant to that of perturbations of certain polynomials of degree at most 2 relies on Green’s function estimates on enlarged resonant blocks of previous induction steps and the Schur complement argument. In the case of Laplacian or (sub)exponential long-range hopping, this reduction can be completed via just iterating the resolvent identities, which turns out to be quite standard. However, if the hopping has a slower power-law decay, this argument of iterating resolvent identities cannot work. To overcome this difficulty, we use ideas from
[
BB13
,
Shi21
]
, in which Green’s function estimates can be established via perturbing the left inverses. This
constructive
method can avoid multiple steps of iterations. Once the zeros were determined via this novel approach that
directly guesses out
the estimates of Green’s functions on enlarged resonant blocks via Green’s function estimates on smaller induction scales enlarged resonant blocks, it remains to handle estimates of Green’s functions on more general
good
subsets that contain no
bad
enlarged resonant blocks of certain induction scales. Again in this procedure, we do not iterate the resolvent identities, but only focus on the left inverses.

While our method here is motivated by
[
BB13
,
Shi21
]
, there are some major differences: (1). Since we are dealing with spectral problems, the resonances between energies and frequencies are inevitable, and we have to establish estimates valid for all Diophantine frequencies, all phases
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
and all
E\in v(\mathbb{D}_{R/2})
. In
[
BB13
]
, the separation property of resonant blocks which is essential for the off-diagonal decay estimates was obtained by removing additional tangential frequencies along the Diophantine directions; (2). The key coupling lemma (cf. Proposition 4.1, page 249 of
[
BB13
]
) cannot be used in the present: Since we are in the case of both QP potentials and non-self-adjoint operators, there is no prior control on the growth of the operator norm (by using eigenvalues variations argument) of Green’s functions on general blocks. To overcome this difficulty, we first establish Green’s function estimates on
good
enlarged resonant blocks (namely, on which the operator norm of the Green’s function can be controlled via roots of the Dirichlet determinant) via taking account of estimates obtained in
all
previous induction scales. Then we cover general
good
sets with
good
enlarged resonant blocks and there is no need to deal with
bad
resonant blocks in this case; (3). Note that the definition of Sobolev norm (
1.5
) in the present is different from that of Berti-Bolle
[
BB13
]
, in which they used the
\ell^{2}
-type norm (cf. Definition 3.2, page 244 of
[
BB13
]
). We do think it is more suitable to use the
\ell^{1}
-type norm of (
1.5
) in the study of spectral problems, while the
\ell^{2}
-type one may be better in the study of nonlinear PDEs. Apart from this consideration, we also give self-contained proofs of all needed estimates concerning our definition.

We believe the method developed in the present paper may have potential applications in proving localization type results for QP Schrödinger operators with more general potentials beyond the analytic cosine type ones.

1.3.
Structure of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. We introduce some important properties concerning Sobolev type norms in §
3
. In §
4
, we establish the quantitative Green’s function estimates via the MSA induction. In §
5
–§
8
, we will apply quantitative Green’s function estimates to prove power-law localization, dynamical localization, the Hölder continuity of the IDS and absence of eigenvalues, respectively. Some useful estimates are given in the appendix.

2.
The notation

•

Given
A,B\geq 0
, we write
A\lesssim B
(resp.
A\gtrsim B
) if there is some
C=C(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0},d,\tau,\gamma,v,R,\phi)>0
depending on
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0},d,\tau,\gamma,v,R,\phi
so that
A\leq CB
(resp.
A\geq CB
). We also define

\displaystyle A\sim B\Leftrightarrow\frac{1}{C}<\frac{A}{B}<C.

•

The determinant of a matrix
M
is denoted by
\det M
.

•

If
a\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\|a\|_{\mathbb{T}}={\rm dist}(a,\mathbb{Z})=\inf\limits_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}|l-a|
. For
z=a+\sqrt{-1}b\in\mathbb{C}
with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, define
\|z\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\sqrt{\|a\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{2}+|b|^{2}}
.

•

For
\bm{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

\displaystyle\|\bm{n}\|=\sup_{1\leq i\leq d}|n_{i}|.

Denoted by
{\rm dist}(\cdot,\cdot)
the distance induced by
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and define

\displaystyle{\rm diam}(\Lambda)=\sup_{\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime}\in\Lambda}\|\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime}\|.

Given
\bm{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
L>0
, define

\displaystyle\Lambda_{L}(\bm{n})=\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \|\bm{k}-\bm{n}\|\leq L\}

and

\displaystyle\Lambda_{L}(\Lambda^{\prime})=\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ {\rm dist}(\bm{k},\Lambda^{\prime})\leq L\}.

In particular, write
\Lambda_{L}=\Lambda_{L}(\bm{0})
.

•

\{\delta_{\bm{x}}\}_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is the standard basis of
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
.

•

\mathcal{I}
typically denotes the identity operator.

•

\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda}
is the restriction operator with
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{T}:\ \ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
be a linear operator. Denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the standard inner product on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
. Set
\mathcal{T}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\langle\delta_{\bm{x}},\mathcal{T}\delta_{\bm{y}}\rangle
. The spectrum of operator
\mathcal{T}
is denoted by
\sigma(\mathcal{T})
. Finally, we define
\mathcal{T}^{\{\bm{k}\}}=\mathcal{R}_{\{\bm{k}\}}\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}=\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{*}
the adjugate operator of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
, where
\{\bm{k}\},\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

•

For
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, denote by
\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda_{2}}^{\Lambda_{1}}
the set of all operators
\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda_{1}}\mathcal{M}\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda_{2}}
with
\mathcal{M}:\ \ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
being linear operators.

•

Define for
\alpha\geq 0
the Sobolev norm of
\psi=\{\psi(\bm{k})\}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
as

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{\alpha}=\sum_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\psi(\bm{k})|(1+\|\bm{k}\|)^{\alpha}.

3.
Preliminaries

In this section, we will introduce some important facts on Sobolev norms (
1.5
).

3.1.
Tame property

The norm defined by (
1.5
) has the following important tame property.

Lemma 3.1

(Tame property)
.

For any
n\geq 1
and
\alpha\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\|\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}_{i}\|_{\alpha}\leq K(n,\alpha)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{j\neq i}\|\mathcal{M}_{j}\|_{0}\right)\|\mathcal{M}_{i}\|_{\alpha},

(3.1)

where

\displaystyle K(n,\alpha)=n^{\max(0,\alpha-1)}\geq 1.

(3.2)

Proof.

We refer to the Appendix
A
for a detailed proof.
∎

3.2.
Smoothing property

The smoothing property plays an essential role in our estimates. In the present, we have

Lemma 3.2

(Smoothing property)
.

For
\alpha\geq\alpha^{\prime}\geq 0
,
N\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime})=0\ \text{\rm for}\ \|\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime}\|\leq N\ \Rightarrow\ \|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha^{\prime}}\leq(1+N)^{-(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime})}\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha},

(3.3)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime})=0\ \text{\rm for}\ \|\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime}\|\geq N\ \Rightarrow\ \|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha}\leq(1+N)^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha^{\prime}}.

(3.4)

Proof.

By the definition (
1.5
), if
\mathcal{M}(\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime})=0\ \text{for}\ \|\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime}\|\leq N
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\|\bm{k}\|>N}\left(\sup_{\bm{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\mathcal{M}(\bm{k}+\bm{l},\bm{l})|\right)(1+\|\bm{k}\|)^{\alpha^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+N)^{-(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime})}\sum_{\|\bm{k}\|>N}\left(\sup_{\bm{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\mathcal{M}(\bm{k}+\bm{l},\bm{l})|\right)(1+\|\bm{k}\|)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(1+N)^{-(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime})}\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha}.

Similarly, we can obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha}\leq(1+N)^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha^{\prime}}

if
\mathcal{M}(\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime})=0\ \text{for}\ \|\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime}\|\geq N
.
∎

We also need the rows estimate

Lemma 3.3

(Rows estimate)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\in\mathbf{M}_{C}^{B}
. Then for all
\alpha_{0}>d
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha}\leq B_{1}(\alpha_{0})\max\limits_{\bm{k}\in B}\|\mathcal{M}^{\{\bm{k}\}}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}},

(3.5)

where
B_{1}(\alpha_{0})=\sum\limits_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(1+\|\bm{k}\|)^{-\alpha_{0}}
.

Proof.

For
\bm{k}\in B,\bm{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\alpha_{0}>d
, we get

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}(\bm{k},\bm{i})|\leq\frac{\|\mathcal{M}^{\{\bm{k}\}}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}}{(1+\|\bm{k}-\bm{i}\|)^{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}}\leq\frac{\max\limits_{\bm{k}\in B}\|\mathcal{M}^{\{\bm{k}\}}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}}{(1+\|\bm{k}-\bm{i}\|)^{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}}.

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left(\sup_{\bm{k}\in B}|\mathcal{M}(\bm{k},\bm{k}-\bm{l})|\right)(1+\|\bm{l}\|)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq\max\limits_{\bm{k}\in B}\|\mathcal{M}^{\{\bm{k}\}}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}\left(\sum_{\bm{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(1+\|\bm{l}\|)^{-\alpha_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=B_{1}(\alpha_{0})\max\limits_{\bm{k}\in B}\|\mathcal{M}^{\{\bm{k}\}}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}.

∎

3.3.
Perturbation argument

It will be convenient to use the notion of left invertible operators.

Definition 3.4

(Left inverse)
.

An operator
\mathcal{M}\in\mathbf{M}_{B}^{C}
is left invertible if there is some
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbf{M}_{C}^{B}
such that
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{I}_{B}
. Then
\mathcal{N}
is called a
left inverse
of
\mathcal{M}
.

We shall often use the following perturbation lemma concerning left invertible operators.

Lemma 3.5

(Perturbation argument)
.

If
\mathcal{M}\in\mathbf{M}_{B}^{C}
has a left inverse
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbf{M}_{C}^{B}
, then for all
\mathcal{P}\in\mathbf{M}_{B}^{C}
with
\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, the operator
\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{P}
has a left inverse
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{P}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{P}}\|_{0}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0},

(3.6)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{P}}\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq B_{2}(\alpha)\left(\|\mathcal{N}\|_{\alpha}+\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}^{2}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{\alpha}\right),

(3.7)

where
B_{2}(\alpha)=K(2,\alpha)\left(3+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{K(2i,\alpha)}{2^{i-1}}\right)
and
K(n,\alpha)
is defined by (
3.2
).

Proof.

Since
\|\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}\|_{0}\leq\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}
by (
3.1
), we have via using the Neumann series argument

\displaystyle(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{-1}=\mathcal{I}_{B}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(-\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{i},

and again by (
3.1
),

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\|\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P}\|_{0})^{i}\leq 2,

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{-1}\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}K(2i,\alpha)\left(\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}^{i}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{0}^{i-1}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{\alpha}+\|\mathcal{P}\|_{0}^{i}\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}^{i-1}\|\mathcal{N}\|_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{K(2i,\alpha)}{2^{i-1}}\left(\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{\alpha}+\|\mathcal{N}\|_{\alpha}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{0}\right).

Set
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{P}}=(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{-1}\mathcal{N}
. We get

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{-1}(\mathcal{N}\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{-1}(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{I}_{B},

which implies
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a left inverse of
\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{P}
. According to (
3.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{P}}\|_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{-1}\|_{0}\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}\leq 2\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{P}}\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq K(2,\alpha)\left(\|(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{-1}\|_{0}\|\mathcal{N}\|_{\alpha}+\|(\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{N}\mathcal{P})^{-1}\|_{\alpha}\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq K(2,\alpha)\left(\left(2+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{K(2i,\alpha)}{2^{i}}\right)\|N\|_{\alpha}+\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{K(2i,\alpha)}{2^{i-1}}\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}^{2}\|P\|_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq K(2,\alpha)\left(3+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{K(2i,\alpha)}{2^{i-1}}\right)\left(\|\mathcal{N}\|_{\alpha}+\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}^{2}\|\mathcal{P}\|_{\alpha}\right).

This finishes the proof.
∎

3.4.
Hadamard type estimate

We need to estimate
0
-norm of the inverse of some operator
\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}
. By the Cramer’s rule,
\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}^{-1}=(\det\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda})^{-1}S_{\Lambda}^{*}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}^{*}(\bm{i},\bm{j})
is a determinant for
\bm{i},\bm{j}\in\Lambda
. Therefore, we can apply Hadamard’s inequality to estimate
0
-norm of
\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}^{*}
and thus that of
\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}^{-1}
.

Lemma 3.6

(Hadamard’s estimate)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}:\ \ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})\to\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
be a linear operator and let
\Lambda
be a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Then for any
\bm{i},\bm{j}\in\Lambda
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle\delta_{\bm{i}},\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}^{*}\delta_{\bm{j}}\rangle|\leq\|\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}\|_{0}^{\#\Lambda-1}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}^{*}\|_{0}\leq(\#\Lambda)^{2}\|\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}\|_{0}^{\#\Lambda-1}.

Proof.

We refer to the Appendix
A
for a detailed proof.
∎

3.5.
Off-diagonal decay of Green’s functions

Lemma 3.7
.

Assume
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\mathcal{F}:\ \ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})\rightarrow\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
is a linear operator. If
\bm{n}\in\Lambda
and
{\rm dist}(\bm{n},\Lambda^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{100}{\rm diam}(\Lambda)
, then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{n}^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}|\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}(\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime})|\lesssim({\rm diam}(\Lambda))^{-\alpha_{1}}\|\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}\|_{\alpha_{1}}.

(3.8)

Proof.

Since (
1.5
) and
{\rm dist}(\bm{n},\Lambda^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{100}{\rm diam}(\Lambda)
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{n}^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}|\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}(\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\lesssim({\rm diam}(\Lambda))^{-\alpha_{1}}\sum_{\bm{n}^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}|\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}(\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime})|(1+\|\bm{n}-\bm{n}^{\prime}\|)^{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq({\rm diam}(\Lambda))^{-\alpha_{1}}\sum_{\bm{n}^{\prime}\in\Lambda}|\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}(\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime})|(1+\|\bm{n}-\bm{n}^{\prime}\|)^{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq({\rm diam}(\Lambda))^{-\alpha_{1}}\|\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}\|_{\alpha_{1}}.

∎

4.
Quantitative Green’s function estimates

In this section, we fix

\displaystyle\theta\in\mathbb{D}_{R/2},\ E\in v(\mathbb{D}_{R/2}).

Write

\displaystyle E=v(\theta_{0})

(4.1)

for some
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{D}_{R/2}
. Consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(E;\theta)=\mathcal{H}(\theta)-E=\mathcal{D}+\varepsilon\mathcal{W}_{\phi},

(4.2)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{\bm{n}}\delta_{\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime}},\ \mathcal{D}_{\bm{n}}=v(\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})-E.

For simplicity, we may omit the dependence of
\mathcal{T}(E;\theta)
on
E,\theta
and that of
\mathcal{W}_{\phi}
on
\phi
, respectively.

Now we introduce the statement of our main result on the MSA type Green’s function estimates. Define the induction parameters

\displaystyle N_{s+1}=\left[\left(\frac{\gamma}{\delta_{s}}\right)^{\frac{1}{30\tau}}\right],\ \frac{\gamma}{\delta_{s+1}}=\left(\frac{\gamma}{\delta_{s}}\right)^{30},\ \delta_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{30}}.

(4.3)

We first introduce the following induction statement.

Statement 4.1

(called
\mathscr{P}_{s}\ (s\geq 1)
)
.

Let

\displaystyle Q_{s-1}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in P_{s-1}:\ \|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{s-1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{s-1}\},\ Q_{s-1}=Q_{s-1}^{+}\cup Q_{s-1}^{-},

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{s-1}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in P_{s-1}:\ \|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{s-1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{s-1}^{\frac{2}{3}}\},\ \tilde{Q}_{s-1}=\tilde{Q}_{s-1}^{+}\cup\tilde{Q}_{s-1}^{-}.

We distinguish two cases:

\displaystyle(\bm{C}1)_{s-1}:\ {\rm dist}(\tilde{Q}_{s-1}^{-},Q_{s-1}^{+})>100N_{s}^{3}

(4.4)

and

\displaystyle(\bm{C}2)_{s-1}:\ {\rm dist}(\tilde{Q}_{s-1}^{-},Q_{s-1}^{+})\leq 100N_{s}^{3}.

(4.5)

Let

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{d}\ni\bm{l}_{s-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\bm{0},&\text{if \eqref{c1} holds true},\\
\bm{i}_{s-1}-\bm{j}_{s-1},&\text{if \eqref{c2} holds true},\end{array}\right.

where
\bm{i}_{s-1}\in Q_{s-1}^{+}
and
\bm{j}_{s-1}\in\tilde{Q}_{s-1}^{-}
such that
\|\bm{i}_{s-1}-\bm{j}_{s-1}\|\leq 100N_{s}^{3}
in
(\bm{C}2)_{s-1}
. Set
\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{0}=\{\bm{k}\}\ (\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d})
. Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be a finite set. We say
\Lambda
is
(s-1)
-
{\rm good}
iff

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\bm{k}^{\prime}\in Q_{s^{\prime}},\ \tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}\subset\Lambda,\ \tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}\subset\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s^{\prime}+1}\Rightarrow\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s^{\prime}+1}\subset\Lambda,\ \text{for}\ s^{\prime}<s-1,\\
\{\bm{k}\in P_{s-1}:\ \tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s-1}\subset\Lambda\}\cap Q_{s-1}=\emptyset.\end{array}\right.

Then we have

(\bm{a})_{s}:
There is
P_{s}\subset\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
so that the following holds true. In the case of
(\bm{C}1)_{s-1}
, we have

\displaystyle P_{s}=Q_{s-1}\subset\left\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}\bm{l}_{i}:\ \min_{\sigma=\pm 1}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\sigma\theta_{s-1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{s-1}\right\}.

(4.6)

For the case of
(\bm{C}2)_{s-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}P_{s}\subset\left\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}+\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s-1}\bm{l}_{i}:\ \|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<3\delta_{s-1}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right\},\\
\text{or}\ P_{s}\subset\left\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}+\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{s-1}\bm{l}_{i}:\ \|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\frac{1}{2}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<3\delta_{s-1}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right\}.\end{array}

For every
\bm{k}\in P_{s}
, we can find a resonant block
\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and the enlarged resonant block
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with the following properties. If (
4.4
) holds true, then

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N_{s}}(\bm{k})\subset\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\Lambda_{N_{s}+50N_{s-1}^{5}}(\bm{k}),

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N_{s}^{3}}(\bm{k})\subset\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\Lambda_{N_{s}^{3}+50N_{s-1}^{5}}(\bm{k}),

and if (
4.5
) holds true, then

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N_{s}^{3}}(\bm{k})\subset\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\Lambda_{N_{s}^{3}+50N_{s-1}^{5}}(\bm{k}),

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N_{s}^{5}}(\bm{k})\subset\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\Lambda_{N_{s}^{5}+50N_{s-1}^{5}}(\bm{k}).

These resonant blocks are constructed to satisfy the following two properties:

(\bm{a}1)_{s}
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s}\cap\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset\ (s^{\prime}<s)\Rightarrow\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}\subset\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s},\\
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\cap\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset\ (s^{\prime}<s)\Rightarrow\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}\subset\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s},\\
{\rm dist}(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s},\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s})>10{\rm diam}(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s})\ \text{for}\ \bm{k}\neq\bm{k}^{\prime}\in P_{s}.\end{array}\right.

(\bm{a}2)_{s}
: The translation of
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}-\bm{k}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}\bm{l}_{i},

is independent of
\bm{k}\in P_{s}
and symmetrical about the origin.

We denote

\displaystyle\zeta_{s}={\rm diam}(\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s}),\ \tilde{\zeta}_{s}={\rm diam}(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}).

(4.12)

(\bm{b})_{s}
:
Q_{s-1}
is covered by
\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s}\ (\bm{k}\in P_{s})
in the sense that for every
\bm{k}^{\prime}\in Q_{s-1}
, there exists a
\bm{k}\in P_{s}
such that

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s-1}\subset\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s}.

(4.13)

(\bm{c})_{s}
: For each
\bm{k}\in P_{s}
,
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}
contains a subset
A_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s}
with
\#A_{\bm{k}}^{s}\leq 2^{s}
such that
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\setminus A_{\bm{k}}^{s}
is
(s-1)
-
{\rm good}
. Moreover,
A_{\bm{k}}^{s}-\bm{k}
is independent of
\bm{k}
and is symmetrical about the origin.

(\bm{d})_{s}
: There is a
\theta_{s}=\theta_{s}(E)\in\mathbb{C}
with the following properties. Replacing
\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}
by
z+(\bm{n}-\bm{k})\cdot\bm{\omega}
and restricting
z
in

\displaystyle\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ \min_{\sigma=\pm 1}\|z+\sigma\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},

(4.14)

we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{s}(z)=\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}-\bm{k}}(z)=((v(z+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})-E)\delta_{\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime}}+\varepsilon\mathcal{W})_{\bm{n}\in\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}-\bm{k}}.

Then
(\mathcal{M}_{s}(z))_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\setminus A_{\bm{k}}^{s})-\bm{k}}
is invertible and we can define the Schur complement

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{s}(z)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{M}_{s}(z))_{A_{\bm{k}}^{s}-\bm{k}}-\mathcal{R}_{A_{\bm{k}}^{s}-\bm{k}}\mathcal{M}_{s}(z)\mathcal{R}_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\setminus A_{\bm{k}}^{s})-\bm{k}}((\mathcal{M}_{s}(z))_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\setminus A_{\bm{k}}^{s})-\bm{k}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\ \ \times\mathcal{R}_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\setminus A_{\bm{k}}^{s})-\bm{k}}\mathcal{M}_{s}(z)\mathcal{R}_{A_{\bm{k}}^{s}-\bm{k}}.

Moreover, if
z
belongs to the set in (
4.14
), then we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{s}(z)\|_{0}<2|v|_{R}+\sum_{l=0}^{s-1}\delta_{l}<4|v|_{R}

(4.15)

and

\displaystyle\left|\det\mathcal{S}_{s}(z)\right|\gtrsim\delta_{s}^{\frac{2}{75}}\|z-\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\cdot\|z+\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}.

(4.16)

Combining the Schur complement lemma (cf. Lemma
A.2
), we get

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}}^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle<\delta_{s}^{-\frac{1}{15}}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}.

(4.17)

(\bm{e})_{s}
: Let

\displaystyle Q_{s}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in P_{s}:\ \|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{s}\},\ Q_{s}=Q_{s}^{+}\cup Q_{s}^{-},

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{s}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in P_{s}:\ \|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{s}^{\frac{2}{3}}\},\ \tilde{Q}_{s}=\tilde{Q}_{s}^{+}\cup\tilde{Q}_{s}^{-}.

For
\bm{k}\in P_{s}\setminus Q_{s}
and
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}}^{-1}\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle<\zeta_{s}^{\alpha}\delta_{s}^{-\frac{7}{3}}.

(4.18)

We say a finite set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is
s
-
{\rm good}
iff

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\bm{k}^{\prime}\in Q_{s^{\prime}},\ \tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}\subset\Lambda,\ \tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}}\subset\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{s^{\prime}+1}\Rightarrow\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s^{\prime}+1}\subset\Lambda\ \text{for}\ s^{\prime}<s,\\
\{\bm{k}\in P_{s}:\ \tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\Lambda\}\cap Q_{s}=\emptyset.\end{array}\right.

Assume that
\Lambda
is
s
-
{\rm good}
. Then

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle<\delta_{s}^{-\frac{2}{15}}\times\sup\limits_{\{\bm{k}\in P_{s}:\ \tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{s}\subset\Lambda\}}(\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1})

\displaystyle<\delta_{s}^{-\frac{32}{15}},

(4.19)

and for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}],

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{\alpha}<\zeta_{s}^{\alpha}\delta_{s}^{-\frac{14}{3}}.

(4.20)

(\bm{f})_{s}
: We have

\displaystyle\left\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}\bm{l}_{i}:\ \min_{\sigma=\pm 1}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\sigma\theta_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<10\delta_{s}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right\}\subset P_{s}.

(4.21)

The main theorem of this section is

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\bm{\omega}\in DC_{\tau,\gamma}
. Then there is some
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0},d,\tau,\gamma,v,R,\phi)>0
so that for
0<|\varepsilon|<\varepsilon_{0}
, the statement
\mathscr{P}_{s}
holds true for all
s\geq 1
.

The following three subsections are devoted to proving Theorem
4.2
.

4.1.
The initial step

Recalling
v(\theta_{0})=E
,
\mathcal{D}_{\bm{n}}=v(\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})-E
and (
1.3
), we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{D}_{\bm{n}}|=|v(\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})-v(\theta_{0})|\geq\kappa_{1}\|\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}.

Denote

\displaystyle P_{0}=\mathbb{Z}^{d},\ Q_{0}=\{\bm{k}\in P_{0}:\ \min(\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}},\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}})<\delta_{0}\}.

We say a finite set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is
0
-
{\rm good}
iff
\Lambda\cap Q_{0}=\emptyset
.

Lemma 4.3
.

If the set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is
0
-
{\rm good}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-2}.

(4.22)

Proof.

Assuming that
\Lambda
is
0
-good, for
\bm{n}\in\Lambda
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{D}_{\bm{n}}|\geq\kappa_{1}\|\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\kappa_{1}\delta_{0}^{2}.

Then

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{0}=\|\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\leq\kappa_{1}^{-1}\delta_{0}^{-2}.

Applying (
3.1
) implies

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\leq 2^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\varepsilon\|\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\|\mathcal{W}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}<\frac{1}{2}.

Thus

\displaystyle(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}+\varepsilon\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda})^{-1}=\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(-\varepsilon\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda})^{i}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}=(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}+\varepsilon\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda})^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}.

Using (
3.1
) again yields

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}+\varepsilon\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda})^{-1}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}i^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\|\varepsilon\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}^{i}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{i^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}}{2^{i}}\lesssim 1,

and thus

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\leq 2^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\|(\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda}+\varepsilon\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda})^{-1}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\|D_{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-2}.

∎

4.2.
Verification of
\mathscr{P}_{1}

If
\Lambda\cap Q_{0}\neq\emptyset
, then the Neumann series argument of the previous subsection does not work. Thus we use the resolvent identity argument to estimate
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}
whenever
\Lambda
is
1
-
{\rm good}
.

We outline the main steps of the proof. First, we construct resonant blocks
\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{1}\ (\bm{k}\in P_{1})
to cover
Q_{0}
. Second, we use the Schur complement lemma (cf. Lemma
A.2
in the Appendix
A
), Cramer’s rule and Hadamard’s inequality to get

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle<\delta_{0}^{-2}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1},

where
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}
is the enlarged resonant block and
\theta_{1}
is obtained via Rouché’s theorem as one root of the Dirichlet determinant equation
\det\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}(z-\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega})=0
. Next, we use smoothing property, rows estimate and perturbation argument to get for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}],

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle<\zeta_{1}^{\alpha}\delta_{1}^{-\frac{1}{3}}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}

via a
constructive
procedure. Finally, we combine the estimates of
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}
to obtain that of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}^{-1}
in a similar way for more general
1
-
{\rm good}

\Lambda
.

Recall that

\displaystyle N_{1}=\left[\left(\frac{\gamma}{\delta_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{30\tau}}\right]

and define (cf. (
4.1
))

\displaystyle Q_{0}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}\},\ Q_{0}=Q_{0}^{+}\cup Q_{0}^{-},

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{0}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}\},\ \tilde{Q}_{0}=\tilde{Q}_{0}^{+}\cup\tilde{Q}_{0}^{-}.

We distinguish the verification into three steps.

Step 1

:
Estimates of
\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{0}
.

In this step, we will find
\theta_{1}=\theta_{1}(E)
so that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle<\delta_{0}^{-2}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}.

We again divide the discussions into two cases.

Case 1

: The case
(\bm{C}1)_{0}
occurs, i.e.,

\displaystyle\text{dist}\left(\tilde{Q}_{0}^{-},Q_{0}^{+}\right)>100N_{1}^{3}.

(4.23)

Remark 4.1
.

We have in fact

\displaystyle\text{dist}\left(\tilde{Q}_{0}^{-},Q_{0}^{+}\right)=\text{dist}\left(\tilde{Q}_{0}^{+},Q_{0}^{-}\right).

Thus (
4.23
) also implies

\displaystyle\text{dist}\left(\tilde{Q}_{0}^{+},Q_{0}^{-}\right)>100N_{1}^{3}.

We refer to the Appendix
A
for a detailed proof.

Assuming (
4.23
) holds true, we define

\displaystyle P_{1}=Q_{0}=\{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \min(\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}},\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}})<\delta_{0}\}.

(4.24)

Associate each
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
with
\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{1}:=\Lambda_{N_{1}}(\bm{k})
and
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}:=\Lambda_{N_{1}^{3}}(\bm{k})
. Then
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is independent of
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
and symmetrical about the origin. If
\bm{k}\neq\bm{k}^{\prime}\in P_{1}
, then

\displaystyle\|\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime}\|\geq\min\left(100N_{1}^{3},\left(\frac{\gamma}{2\delta_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right)\geq 100N_{1}^{3}.

Thus

\displaystyle\text{dist}\left(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1},\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{1}\right)>10\tilde{\zeta}_{1}\ \text{for}\ \bm{k}\neq\bm{k}^{\prime}\in P_{1}.

For
\bm{k}\in Q_{0}^{-}
, we consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}(z):=\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}}(z)=\left((v(z+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})-E)\delta_{\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime}}+\varepsilon\mathcal{W}\right)_{\bm{n}\in\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}}

defined in

\displaystyle\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ |z-\theta_{0}|<\delta_{0}^{\frac{18}{19}}\right\}.

(4.25)

For
\bm{n}\in\left(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}\right)\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
, we have for
0<\delta_{0}\ll 1
,

\displaystyle\|z+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\geq\|\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}\|_{\mathbb{T}}-|z-\theta_{0}|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\gamma}{(N_{1}^{3})^{\tau}}-\delta_{0}^{\frac{18}{19}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{10}}.

For
\bm{n}\in\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\|z+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\geq\|\theta+(\bm{n}+\bm{k})\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}-|z-\theta_{0}|-\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}-\delta_{0}^{\frac{18}{19}}-\delta_{0}>\frac{1}{2}\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}.

Hence for
\bm{n}\in\left(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}\right)\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
,

\displaystyle|v(z+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})-E|\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{23}{30}}\gg\varepsilon.

By Neumann series argument, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left((\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\{\bm{0}\}}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{0}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-\frac{23}{30}}.

(4.26)

Now, we can apply the Schur complement lemma to establish desired estimates. By Lemma
A.2
,
(\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))^{-1}
is controlled by the Schur complement (of
(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))_{\{\bm{0}\}}-\mathcal{R}_{\{\bm{0}\}}\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)\mathcal{R}_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\{\bm{0}\}}\left((\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\{\bm{0}\}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\ \ \times\mathcal{R}_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\{\bm{0}\}}\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)\mathcal{R}_{\{\bm{0}\}}

\displaystyle=v(z)-E+r(z)=g(z)((z-\theta_{0})+r_{1}(z)),

where
g(z)
and
r_{1}(z)
are analytic functions in the set of (
4.25
) satisfying
|g(z)|>\kappa_{1}\|z+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}>\frac{\kappa_{1}}{2}\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}
and
|r_{1}(z)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\delta_{0}^{-\frac{43}{30}}\ll\delta_{0}^{\frac{18}{19}}
. Since

\displaystyle|r_{1}(z)|<|z-\theta_{0}|\ \text{for}\ |z-\theta_{0}|=\delta_{0}^{\frac{18}{19}},

using the Rouché’s theorem and maximum modulus principle implies

\displaystyle(z-\theta_{0})+r_{1}(z)=0

has a unique root
\theta_{1}
in the set of (
4.25
) satisfying

\displaystyle|\theta_{0}-\theta_{1}|=|r_{1}(\theta_{1})|<\varepsilon,\ |(z-\theta_{0})+r_{1}(z)|\sim|z-\theta_{1}|.

(4.27)

Moreover,
\theta_{1}
is the unique root of
\det\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)=0
in the set of (
4.25
). Since
\|z+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}>\frac{1}{2}\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}
and
|\theta_{0}-\theta_{1}|<\varepsilon
, we get

\displaystyle\|z+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\sim\|z+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}.

Then by (
4.26
) and (
A.2
), we have for
z
being in the set of (
4.25
),

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)|

\displaystyle\gtrsim\|z+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\cdot\|z-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}},

(4.28)

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle<4\left(1+\left\|\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\{\bm{0}\}}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{0}\right)^{2}(1+|\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)|^{-1})

\displaystyle<\delta_{0}^{-2}\|z+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|z-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}.

(4.29)

Now, for
\bm{k}\in Q_{0}^{+}
, we consider
\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)
in the set

\displaystyle\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ |z+\theta_{0}|<\delta_{0}^{\frac{18}{19}}\right\}.

(4.30)

The similar argument shows that
\det\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)=0
has a unique root
\theta_{1}^{\prime}
in the set of (
4.30
). We will show
\theta_{1}+\theta_{1}^{\prime}=0
. In fact, by Lemma
A.3
,
\det\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)
is an even function of
z
. Then the uniqueness of the root implies
\theta_{1}^{\prime}=-\theta_{1}
. Thus for
z
being in the set of (
4.30
), both (
4.28
) and (
4.29
) hold true as well. Finally, (
4.28
) and (
4.29
) remain valid for

\displaystyle\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ \min\limits_{\sigma=\pm 1}|z+\sigma\theta_{0}|<\delta_{0}^{\frac{18}{19}}\}.

(4.31)

From (
4.24
), we have
\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}
belongs to the set of (
4.31
). Thus for
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
, we get

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle=\|(\mathcal{M}_{1}(\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}))^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle<\delta_{0}^{-2}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}.

(4.32)

Case 2

: The case
(\bm{C}2)_{0}
occurs, i.e.,

\displaystyle\text{dist}\left(\tilde{Q}_{0}^{-},Q_{0}^{+}\right)\leq 100N_{1}^{3}.

(4.33)

Then there exist
\bm{i}_{0}\in Q_{0}^{+}
and
\bm{j}_{0}\in\tilde{Q}_{0}^{-}
with
\|\bm{i}_{0}-\bm{j}_{0}\|\leq 100N_{1}^{3}
such that

\displaystyle\|\theta+\bm{i}_{0}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0},\ \|\theta+\bm{j}_{0}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}.

Set
\bm{l}_{0}=\bm{i}_{0}-\bm{j}_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\|\bm{l}_{0}\|=\text{dist}\left(\tilde{Q}_{0}^{-},Q_{0}^{+}\right)=\text{dist}\left(\tilde{Q}_{0}^{+},Q_{0}^{-}\right).

Define

\displaystyle O_{1}=Q_{0}^{-}\cup(Q_{0}^{+}-\bm{l}_{0}).

For
\bm{k}\in Q_{0}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\theta+(\bm{k}-\bm{l}_{0})\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle<\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}+\|\bm{l}_{0}\cdot\bm{\omega}+2\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle<\delta_{0}+\delta_{0}+\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}<2\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}.

Thus

\displaystyle O_{1}\subset\left\{\bm{o}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \|\theta+\bm{o}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<2\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}\right\}.

For every
\bm{o}\in O_{1}
, define its mirror point

\displaystyle\bm{o}^{*}=\bm{o}+\bm{l}_{0}.

Next, define

\displaystyle P_{1}=\left\{\frac{1}{2}(\bm{o}+\bm{o}^{*}):\ \bm{o}\in O_{1}\right\}=\left\{\bm{o}+\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}:\ \bm{o}\in O_{1}\right\}.

(4.34)

Associate each
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
with
\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{1}:=\Lambda_{100N_{1}^{3}}(\bm{k})
and
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}:=\Lambda_{N_{1}^{5}}(\bm{k})
. Thus

\displaystyle Q_{0}\subset\bigcup_{\bm{k}\in P_{1}}\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{1}

and
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}+\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}
is independent of
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
and symmetrical about origin. Notice that

\displaystyle\min

\displaystyle\left(\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}},\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{l}_{0}\cdot\bm{\omega}+2\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}(\|\theta+\bm{i}_{0}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}+\|\theta+\bm{j}_{0}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}})<\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}.

Since
\delta_{0}\ll 1
, only one of

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}\ \text{and}\ \left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}

holds true. First, we consider the case of

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}.

(4.35)

Let
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
. Since
\bm{k}=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{o}+\bm{o}^{*})=\bm{o}+\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}
(for some
\bm{o}\in O_{1}
), we have

\displaystyle\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq\|\theta+\bm{o}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}+\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}<3\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}.

(4.36)

Thus if
\bm{k}\neq\bm{k}^{\prime}\in P_{1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime}\|\geq\left(\frac{\gamma}{6\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\tau}}\sim N_{1}^{20}\gg 100N_{1}^{5},

which implies

\displaystyle\text{dist}\left(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1},\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{1}\right)>10\zeta_{1}\ \text{for}\ \bm{k}\neq\bm{k}^{\prime}\in P_{1}.

Consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}(z):=\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}}(z)=\left((v(z+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})-E)\delta_{\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime}}+\varepsilon\mathcal{W}\right)_{\bm{n}\in\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}}

in the set of

\displaystyle\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ |z|<\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}}\right\}.

(4.37)

For
\bm{n}\neq\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}
and
\bm{n}\in\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\left(\bm{n}\mp\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right)\cdot\bm{\omega}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}-\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle>\frac{\gamma}{(2N_{1}^{5})^{\tau}}-\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{6}}.

Thus for
z
being in the set of (
4.37
) and
\bm{n}\neq\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\|z+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\|\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}-|z|\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{6}}.

Hence for
\bm{n}\in(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\},
we have

\displaystyle|v(z+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})-E|\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{3}}\gg\varepsilon.

Using Neumann series argument concludes

\displaystyle\left\|\left((\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{0}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

(4.38)

Thus by Lemma
A.2
,
(\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))^{-1}
is controlled by the Schur complement of
(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))_{\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}-\mathcal{R}_{\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)\mathcal{R}_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}

\displaystyle\ \ \times\left((\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)\mathcal{R}_{\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}},

where

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{R}_{\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)\mathcal{R}_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\right\|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{R}_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)\mathcal{R}_{\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\right\|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon.

Then by Lemma
A.4
and (
4.38
), we get

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)\|_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|(\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))_{\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\right\|_{0}+O(\varepsilon^{2}\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{3}})

\displaystyle\leq 2|v|_{R}+\delta_{0}<4|v|_{R},

(4.39)

and

\displaystyle\det\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\det\left((\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))_{\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\right)+O(\varepsilon^{2}\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{3}})

\displaystyle=\left(v\left(z+\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}\right)-E\right)\left(v\left(z-\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}\right)-E\right)+O(\varepsilon^{2}\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{3}}).

In the case of
\bm{l}_{0}=\bm{0}
, the argument is easier and we omit the discussion. In the following, we deal with
\bm{l}_{0}\neq\bm{0}
. By (
4.35
) and (
4.37
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|z+\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\geq\|\bm{l}_{0}\cdot\bm{\omega}\|_{\mathbb{T}}-\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}-|z|

\displaystyle>\frac{\gamma}{(100N_{1}^{3})^{\tau}}-\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}-\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{10}},

and

\displaystyle\left\|z-\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\geq\|\bm{l}_{0}\cdot\bm{\omega}\|_{\mathbb{T}}-\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}-|z|

\displaystyle>\frac{\gamma}{(100N_{1}^{3})^{\tau}}-\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}-\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{10}}.

Let
z_{1}
satisfy

\displaystyle z_{1}\equiv\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\ (\text{mod}\ \mathbb{Z}),\ |z_{1}|=\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}.

(4.40)

Then

\displaystyle|\det\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)|

\displaystyle\gtrsim\left\|z+\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}\cdot\left\|z-\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}\cdot|(z-z_{1})(z+z_{1})+r_{1}(z)|

\displaystyle\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{5}}|(z-z_{1})(z+z_{1})+r_{1}(z)|,

where
r_{1}(z)
is an analytic function in the set of (
4.37
) with

\displaystyle|r_{1}(z)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\delta_{0}^{-\frac{8}{15}}\ll\varepsilon\ll\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}}.

(4.41)

Applying Rouché’s theorem shows that

\displaystyle(z-z_{1})(z+z_{1})+r_{1}(z)=0

has exact two roots
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{1}^{\prime}
in the set of (
4.37
), which are perturbations of
\pm z_{1}
. If

\displaystyle|z_{1}-\theta_{1}|>|r_{1}(\theta_{1})|^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \text{and}\ |z_{1}+\theta_{1}|>|r_{1}(\theta_{1})|^{\frac{1}{2}},

then

\displaystyle|r_{1}(\theta_{1})|=|z_{1}-\theta_{1}|\cdot|z_{1}+\theta_{1}|>|r_{1}(\theta_{1})|,

which is a contradiction. Without loss of generality, we assume that

\displaystyle|z_{1}-\theta_{1}|\leq|r_{1}(\theta_{1})|^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Notice that

\displaystyle\left\{|z|<\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}}:\ \det\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)=0\right\}=\left\{|z|<\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}}:\ \det\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)=0\right\}

and
\det\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)
is an even function (cf. Lemma
A.3
) of
z
. Thus

\displaystyle\theta_{1}^{\prime}=-\theta_{1}.

Moreover, since (
4.40
) and (
4.41
), we get for
|z|=\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}},

\displaystyle\frac{|r_{1}(z)-r_{1}(\theta_{1})|}{|(z-z_{1})(z+z_{1})+r_{1}(\theta_{1})|}\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}\delta_{0}^{-\frac{107}{60}},

which combined with
\theta_{1}^{2}-z_{1}^{2}+r_{1}(\theta_{1})=0
shows

\displaystyle\frac{|(z-z_{1})(z+z_{1})+r_{1}(z)|}{|(z-\theta_{1})(z+\theta_{1})|}

\displaystyle=\frac{|(z-z_{1})(z+z_{1})+r_{1}(z)|}{|(z-z_{1})(z+z_{1})+r_{1}(\theta_{1})|}

\displaystyle\in\left[1-2\varepsilon^{2}\delta_{0}^{-\frac{107}{60}},1+2\varepsilon^{2}\delta_{0}^{-\frac{107}{60}}\right].

By the maximum modulus principle, we have

\displaystyle|(z-z_{1})(z+z_{1})+r_{1}(z)|\sim|(z-\theta_{1})(z+\theta_{1})|.

Thus for
z
being in the set of (
4.37
), we have

\displaystyle|\det\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)|\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{5}}\|z-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\cdot\|z+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}.

(4.42)

By the Cramer’s rule, Lemma
3.6
, (
4.39
) and (
4.42
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{S}_{1}(z))^{-1}\|_{0}=\frac{\|(\mathcal{S}_{1}(z))^{*}\|_{0}}{|\det\mathcal{S}_{1}(z)|}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{5}}\|z-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|z+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}.

(4.43)

Recalling (
4.38
) and (
4.43
), we get since Lemma
A.2

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle<4\left(1+\left\|\left((\mathcal{M}_{1}(z))_{(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k})\setminus\left\{\pm\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\right\}}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{0}\right)^{2}(1+\|(\mathcal{S}_{1}(z))^{-1}\|_{0})

\displaystyle<\delta_{0}^{-2}\|z-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|z+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}.

(4.44)

Thus for (
4.35
), both (
4.43
) and (
4.44
) are established for
z
belonging to

\displaystyle\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ \|z\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}}\}

since
\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)
is
1
-periodic (in
z
). By (
4.36
), for
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
, we also have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle=\|(\mathcal{M}_{1}(\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}))^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle<\delta_{0}^{-2}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}.

For the case of

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\bm{l}_{0}}{2}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}},

(4.45)

we have for
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\frac{1}{2}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}<3\delta_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}}.

(4.46)

Consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}(z):=\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}}(z)=\left((v(z+\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\omega})-E)\delta_{\bm{n},\bm{n}^{\prime}}+\varepsilon\mathcal{W}\right)_{\bm{n}\in\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}-\bm{k}}

in

\displaystyle\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ \left|z-\frac{1}{2}\right|<\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}}\right\}.

(4.47)

The similar argument shows that
\det\mathcal{M}_{1}(z)=0
has two roots
\theta_{1}
and
\theta^{\prime}_{1}
in the set of (
4.47
) such that (
4.39
)–(
4.44
) hold true for
z
being in the set of (
4.47
).
Hence if (
4.45
), then (
4.39
)–(
4.44
) hold for
z
being in

\displaystyle\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ \left\|z-\frac{1}{2}\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{0}^{\frac{5}{8}}\}.

By (
4.46
), for
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
, we also have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle=\|(\mathcal{M}_{1}(\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}))^{-1}\|_{0}

\displaystyle<\delta_{0}^{-2}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}.

(4.48)

Therefore, we have established desired estimates on
\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{0}
for both cases of
(\bm{C}1)_{0}
and
(\bm{C}2)_{0}
.

Step 2

:
Estimates of
\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}].

The main result of this step is Theorem
4.4
below, which says that even though there is some singular sites of
0
-scale in the block
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}
can become controllable if there is no
1
-scale singular sites. Recalling

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\gamma}{\delta_{1}}\right)=\left(\frac{\gamma}{\delta_{0}}\right)^{30}

and

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{\pm}=\{\bm{k}\in P_{1}:\ \|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}\pm\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\delta_{1}\},\ Q_{1}=Q_{1}^{+}\cup Q_{1}^{-},

we have

Theorem 4.4
.

For
\bm{k}\in P_{1}\setminus Q_{1}
and
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{\alpha}<\zeta_{1}^{\alpha}\delta_{1}^{-\frac{7}{3}}.

(4.49)

The proof of theorem
4.4
builds on several lemmas. The next Lemmas
4.5
and
4.6
say that we can construct
\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}
by some operators with good controls of
\alpha
-norm directly. In each of them, we use the smoothing property (cf. Lemma
3.2
) to control norms and use the perturbation argument (cf. Lemma
3.5
) to construct inverse operators.

For
\bm{k}\in P_{1}
, we define
A_{\bm{k}}^{1}\subset\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{1}
to be

\displaystyle A_{\bm{k}}^{1}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\{\bm{k}\}&\text{case}\ (\bm{C}1)_{0}\\
\{\bm{o}\}\cup\{\bm{o}^{*}\}&\text{case}\ (\bm{C}2)_{0}\end{array}\right.,

where
\bm{k}=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{o}+\bm{o}^{*})
for some
\bm{o}\in O_{1}
in the case of
(\bm{C}2)_{0}
.

Let
G=\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}\setminus\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{1}
and
B=\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{1}
. Thus
X:=\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}=B\cup G
,
G\cap A_{\bm{k}}^{1}=\emptyset
and
A_{\bm{k}}^{1}\subset B
.
We have

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
0<\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0},d,\tau,\gamma,v,R,\phi)\ll 1
. Then there exist
\mathcal{M}\in\mathbf{M}_{X}^{G}
and
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbf{M}_{B}^{G}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\mathcal{T}_{X}=\mathcal{R}_{G}+\mathcal{N}

(4.50)

with the following estimates:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{N}\|_{0}\lesssim N_{1}^{-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}},\ \|\mathcal{M}\|_{0}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-2},

and for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}]
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{N}\|_{\alpha}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{28}<1,\ \|\mathcal{M}\|_{\alpha}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-2}.

(4.51)

Proof.

From our construction, we have

\displaystyle Q_{0}\subset\bigcup_{\bm{k}\in P_{1}}A_{\bm{k}}^{1}\subset\bigcup_{\bm{k}\in P_{1}}\Omega_{\bm{k}}^{1}.

Thus

\displaystyle(\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}\setminus A_{\bm{k}}^{1})\cap Q_{0}=\emptyset,

which shows that
\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}\setminus A_{\bm{k}}^{1}
is
0
-good. Since (
4.48
) and
\bm{k}\notin Q_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}_{\tilde{\Omega}_{\bm{k}}^{1}}^{-1}\|_{0}<\delta_{0}^{-2}\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}-\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\theta+\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\omega}+\theta_{1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\lesssim\delta_{1}^{-\frac{31}{15}}.

(4.52)

Fix
\bm{l}\in G
. Then there exists a
0
-
{\rm good}
set
U_{\bm{l}}=\Lambda_{\frac{N_{1}}{2}}(\bm{l})\cap X
such that
\bm{l}\in U_{\bm{l}}
,
{\rm dist}(\bm{l},X\setminus U_{\bm{l}})\geq\frac{N_{1}}{2}.
Define
\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{l}}=\varepsilon\mathcal{T}_{U_{\bm{l}}}^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{U_{\bm{l}}}\mathcal{W}\mathcal{R}_{X\setminus U_{\bm{l}}}\in\mathbf{M}_{X\setminus U_{\bm{l}}}^{U_{\bm{l}}}
. Using (
3.1
) leads to

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{l}}\|_{\alpha_{1}}\lesssim\varepsilon\|\mathcal{T}_{U_{\bm{l}}}^{-1}\|_{\alpha_{1}}\|\mathcal{W}_{X}\|_{\alpha_{1}}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{28}<1.

(4.53)

By (
3.1
) and (
4.22
), we have for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}],

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{l}}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\left(\|\mathcal{T}_{U_{\bm{l}}}^{-1}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}\|\mathcal{W}_{X}\|_{0}+\|\mathcal{T}_{U_{\bm{l}}}^{-1}\|_{0}\|\mathcal{W}_{X}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\|\mathcal{W}_{X}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\|\mathcal{T}_{U_{\bm{l}}}^{-1}\|_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{28}<1.

(4.54)

We now vary
\bm{l}\in G
. Define
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}\in\mathbf{M}_{X}^{G}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\bm{l},\bm{l}^{\prime})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0,&\text{for}\ \bm{l}^{\prime}\in U_{\bm{l}}\\
\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{l}}(\bm{l},\bm{l}^{\prime}),&\text{for}\ \bm{l}^{\prime}\in X\setminus U_{\bm{l}},\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\bm{l},\bm{l}^{\prime})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{T}_{U_{\bm{l}}}^{-1}(\bm{l},\bm{l}^{\prime}),&\text{for}\ \bm{l}^{\prime}\in U_{\bm{l}}\\
0,&\text{for}\ \bm{l}^{\prime}\in X\setminus U_{\bm{l}}.\end{array}\right.

Direct computation yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\mathcal{T}_{X}=\mathcal{R}_{G}+\mathcal{K}.

(4.55)

We estimate
\mathcal{K}\in\mathbf{M}_{X}^{G}
. Fix
\bm{l}\in G
. Note that if
\bm{l}^{\prime}\in X\setminus U_{\bm{l}}
, then
\|\bm{l}-\bm{l}^{\prime}\|\geq\frac{N_{1}}{2}
. This implies
\mathcal{K}^{\{\bm{l}\}}(\bm{l},\bm{l}^{\prime})=0
for
\|\bm{l}-\bm{l}^{\prime}\|<\frac{N_{1}}{2}
. By (
3.3
), (
3.5
) and (
4.53
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}\|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{\bm{l}\in G}\|\mathcal{K}^{\{\bm{l}\}}\|_{\alpha_{0}}\lesssim N_{1}^{-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\sup_{\bm{l}\in G}\|\mathcal{K}^{\{\bm{l}\}}\|_{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq N_{1}^{-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}\sup_{\bm{l}\in G}\|\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{l}}\|_{\alpha_{1}}\leq N_{1}^{-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}}.

(4.56)

Similarly, by recalling (
3.5
) and (
4.54
), we obtain for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha_{1}],

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{\bm{l}\in G}\|\mathcal{K}^{\{\bm{l}\}}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}\leq\sup_{\bm{l}\in G}\|\mathcal{Q}_{\bm{l}}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{28}<1.

(4.57)

We then estimate
\mathcal{L}\in\mathbf{M}_{X}^{G}
. By (
3.5
) and (
4.22
), we have for
\alpha\in[0,\alpha_{1}]
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{\bm{l}\in G}\|\mathcal{L}^{\{\bm{l}\}}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}\leq\sup_{\bm{l}\in G}\|T_{U_{\bm{l}}}^{-1}\|_{\alpha+\alpha_{0}}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-2}.

(4.58)

Since
N_{1}\gg 1
and
-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}<0
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}\|_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Recalling Lemma
3.5
, we have that
\mathcal{I}_{G}+\mathcal{K}_{G}
is invertible and

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{I}_{G}+\mathcal{K}_{G})^{-1}\|_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\alpha}<1\ \text{for $\alpha\in[0,\alpha_{1}]$}.

(4.59)

From (
4.55
), we have
